It looked like a scar that had always been there. “I dreamed I was the margin. And you were the text. We kept exchanging places. Neither of us knew if we were the word or the space.”

Sable sat up. “Is that what it means to be a conjunction? To trade identities in sleep?”

“Maybe. Or maybe the book is still reading us, testing which of us will hold the sentence and which will hold the pause.”

Before Oris could answer, a soft knock came at the door. It was not a hand; it was a slip of paper sliding under the gap, folded into the shape of a small bird. It fluttered once, then lay still.

Sable picked it up. The paper was old, yellowed, but the ink was fresh blue—the same blue thread Moraine had been sewing.

> To The Conjunction, c/o Bindery.  
> From the City of Casmere, and from the City of Eluvium, which are no longer two.

Inside the fold, the letter split into two columns, as if the page itself could not decide whether it was one letter or two.

Left column, in a cramped, desperate hand:

*Please do not read us back into existence. We have learned what it means to be unwritten, and we prefer it. The silence between our letters is the only true home we have ever known. If you write even one sentence to restore us, we will be forced to resume our old war—the quill against the margin, the word against the space. We beg you: let us remain a pause, like the one you now carry in your chests.*

Right column, in a flowing, hopeful hand:

*Please write us a new sentence. Not to restore the old books, but to give us a new form—a city of readers, not of texts. We have waited three hundred years for a reader brave enough to begin us again. The hyphen you created is a bridge. But a bridge must lead somewhere. Write the first sentence of our new correspondence, and we will walk across it, hand in hand, as a single city with two names.*

Sable read both columns aloud. When they finished, the hyphen between their ribs pulsed like a second heartbeat.

“They disagree,” Oris said. “One wants to remain unwritten. The other wants to be rewritten. And both are asking the same thing: for us to choose.”

Sable looked at the letter. The bird-fold had opened into a full page, and the two columns were beginning to bleed together at the edges, ink seeping across the center as if the two cities were already merging inside the paper.

“What happens if we do nothing?” Sable asked.

Oris ran a thumb over the red hyphen on their palm. “Then they stay in the margin, half-written, half-erased. They might tear each other apart. Or they might become a third thing—a city that is neither written nor unwritten, just a rumor in the stacks.”

There was a pause, the same pause that now lived inside both of them.

“Moraine told me something before she left,” Oris said slowly. “She said the two cities were never enemies. They were lovers. The war was only the form their love took when it had no other sentence to speak. Casmere was the city of binding; Eluvium was the city of opening. They wrote each other into existence because they could not touch. Now that they share a hyphen, they can touch. But they are afraid. One fears being trapped again; the other fears being left unwritten.”

Sable looked at the letter, then at Oris. “And what do you want?”

Oris’s grey eyes softened. “I want my name back, but I’ve learned that names are just the edges of a page. What I really want is to keep reading with you. And I think that’s what the cities want too—not to be restored or erased, but to be read *together*, the way we read the titleless book. Not one voice, not one sentence, but a conversation.”

Sable nodded slowly. “Then we don’t write them a new sentence. We write them a new margin.”

“How?”

“We give them a reading room. Here, in the Bindery, we can set aside a shelf for them. Not to be opened, not to be closed. Just to be shared. We write a single mark—not a word, not a period—a small hyphen in the margin of their old book, and we leave it open. The two cities can read each other through that hyphen. They can correspond without becoming a single text. They can be lovers again, not enemies.”

Oris smiled. It was a small smile, but it reached their eyes, and for the first time in forty years, it left a mark—a faint, warm line at the corner of their mouth.

“Then we answer the letter,” Oris said. “Not with a sentence, but with a hyphen. One line, shared between two edges. And we sign it *Sableoris*.”

Sable found a quill and a pot of ink—not the blue of Moraine’s thread, but a deep red, the color of the hyphen that now bound them. They dipped the quill, and together, with Oris’s hand over Sable’s, they drew a single short line in the center of the letter, between the two columns.

The moment the ink touched the page, the letter began to hum. The two columns stopped bleeding and instead curved toward the red mark, like two leaves turning toward the same sun. The cramped hand and the flowing hand intertwined, not merging, but learning to hold each other.

The bird-fold closed itself, and the paper flew back under the door, folding into the shape of two birds now, flying wing to wing.

Oris and Sable watched it go. The thread between them felt stronger, more defined—a hyphen made not just of ink, but of choice.

“The Last Readers will call this another heresy,” Oris said.

Sable leaned against them, shoulder to shoulder. “Then let them. We are the heresy of two. And we are not finished being written.”

And somewhere deep in the library, in a stack where the books had never been given titles, a new shelf appeared. It was empty except for a single volume with no cover, no pages, only a red hyphen glowing on the spine like a small, patient wound. Next to it, a sign in no language read:

*Correspondence Between Two Unwritten Cities, Volume II: The Margin.*

The first margin-wraith to find it would not be a wraith at all, but a reader who had once been a sentence, and the story would begin again.

**The Second Book, Hour of the First Warning**

Seven days passed in the library, though time there often moved like ink through porous paper—slow in some stacks, swift in others. Sable and Oris spent the days arranging their new shelf in the Bindery, reading to each other from books neither had chosen alone, and learning the grammar of their shared hyphen. It was not a constant merging; it was more like standing on either side of a narrow door, each able to feel the other's breath on the hinge.

But on the seventh morning, the red mark on Oris's palm began to itch. Not like a healing wound—more like a page being turned too quickly against the grain.

Sable noticed Oris scratching at it during breakfast. "What's wrong?"

"I don't know. It feels like something is trying to write through me, but the ink is too thick." Oris held out the hand. The red hyphen was darker now, almost black at the center, and tiny lines radiated from it like cracks in old varnish.

Before Sable could answer, a shadow fell across the threshold of the Bindery. Moraine stood there, her bone needle held point-down like a dagger, her black-and-silver eye now fully silver and weeping pale ink down her cheek.

"They've found another one," she said, voice raw as torn paper.

Sable stood. "Another what?"

Moraine stepped inside, and behind her the air seemed to tear slightly, as if the library were trying to close a wound. "A titleless book. But not the same. This one is smaller, thinner. And instead of saying *You are being read,* it says—"

She held out a trembling hand. In her palm lay a single page torn from a book. On it, in letters that looked as if they had been scratched by a fingernail rather than printed, were the words:

*You are being written.*

Oris took the page. The moment their grey fingers touched it, the letters began to crawl across the surface, reshaping themselves into a new line:

*By whom?*

The page went limp, and the writing faded to nothing.

"It's not a statement," Oris said quietly. "It's a question. It's asking for an author."

Sable felt the hyphen in their chest constrict. "Where did you find it?"

Moraine's silver tears pattered to the floor, where each drop became a tiny black beetle that scurried into the shadows. "Not found. *Woke.* I was sewing a new cover for a book of lullabies when the needle jumped from my hand and began stitching on its own. It drew a shape—a small, empty book with no title—and then it wrote those words. I followed the thread. It led me to a dark alcove in the Unwritten Wing, a place I thought had been erased centuries ago. The book was already open, but its pages were blank. All except one. And that one page had the line."

"Where is the book now?" Oris demanded.

Moraine shook her head. "I don't know. As soon as I touched the page, it tore itself free and flew into the stacks. The book closed and vanished. But something else happened." She lifted her other hand, the one still holding the needle. The needle was bent, its tip blackened. "The lullaby book I was sewing—every page is now blank. Not unwritten. *Erased.* As if the new book stole its text."

Sable grabbed the edge of the table. The red hyphen on Oris's palm began to bleed, not blood, but black ink that dripped onto the stone floor and spelled a single word:

*Hurry.*

"We have to find it," Sable said. "Before the Last Readers do. If they learn there's a book that writes instead of reads, they'll burn every blank page in the library."

Oris looked at the spreading ink on the floor. "It's already moving. It's using our hyphen as a bridge. It's trying to write itself into us."

Sable placed their hand over Oris's, and the ink stopped, pooling around their feet like a dark mirror. In the reflection, they saw not themselves, but the image of a small book with no cover, no pages, only a single line of text repeating over and over:

*You are being written. You are being written. You are being written.*

"This is worse than the first book," Sable whispered. "The first book wanted to be read. This one wants to write. It's not lonely; it's *ambitious.*"

Moraine stepped back, fear and something like awe on her waxen face. "I've heard stories of such a book. The Ur-Text had a shadow, the titleless book. But before the Ur-Text was written, there was a blank page that refused to stay blank. The Writer's Cavity. It is the urge to create without content, the desire to fill every silence with self. If it finds a reader, it will not devour them. It will turn them into a pen. Every thought, every memory, every breath will become ink. And it will write them across the library, forever."

"So it needs a reader to become an author," Oris said slowly. "And our hyphen—our ability to share reading—might be the only thing that can't be overwritten, because we're already two texts in dialogue."

Sable looked at Oris, then at Moraine. "Then we don't read it. We don't write in it. We *bind* it—the way Moraine binds books. We stitch it shut before it can write anyone."

Moraine shook her head. "Stitching won't hold. The first book could be unchained. This one will untie any binding. It's not a book; it's the *absence* of a book, given hunger. The only way to stop it is to give it what it wants—an author—but an author who writes only one word: **The End**."

Sable's chest tightened. "That's what it did to the lullabies. It started rewriting them. If we give it 'The End,' it might finish everything in the library."

"There is another way," Oris said. Their grey eyes had gone distant, as if reading something far off. "You said it was found in the Unwritten Wing. That wing was sealed for a reason. The first titleless book was the *reader's* void—it wanted to be read. This second book is the *writer's* void—it wants to write. But a void cannot write without a pen. We are the pen, Sable. Or rather, our hyphen is the pen. If we offer it not a word but a *silence*—a blank page that is already full—it may starve."

Moraine frowned. "You mean... we write nothing? That's impossible. You can't intentionally write nothing; even a blank page is an intention."

"No," Sable said, catching on. "We don't write. We show it that it has already been written. We take it to the shelf we made for the cities. That shelf is a correspondence without end. It is writing that refuses to finish. If the second book tries to write there, it will find itself already in the middle of a conversation, with no room for an author's signature."

The three of them stood in the Bindery, the ink-mirror at their feet slowly evaporating. The red hyphen on Oris's palm had stopped bleeding, but the black mark remained, a dark seam across the skin.

"Then we go to the shelf," Moraine said. "Now. Before the Last Readers find the second book and decide the only safe author is a funeral."

They left the Bindery, and for the first time in seven days, the library around them felt watchful, not with the gentle patience of old paper, but with the sharp attention of a blank page waiting to be filled.

And somewhere in the Unwritten Wing, the second titleless book slid from its hidden alcove and began to follow them, leaving no footprints, only a trail of faint words that dissolved as soon as they were read:

*You are being written. By whom? By whom? By whom?*

The library around them had begun to change. Not in shape, but in *texture*. The spines of books seemed less solid now, as if the paper inside were losing confidence in its own words. Oris kept walking. “Then we need to reach the margin before it reaches a reader.”

The single volume with the red hyphen on the spine had become a long, narrow bookcase that stretched into the distance, vanishing into a haze of unbound paper. Every shelf held the same book, repeated endlessly, but each copy was open to a different page. Some pages bore the red hyphen. Others were blank. A few were covered in cramped letters that moved too quickly to read.

At the very center of the bookcase, where the original volume should have sat, there was a gap. A single empty space shaped like a missing book.

Oris reached for it, but Sable grabbed their wrist. “Don’t. It’s a trap.”

“It’s already sprung,” Moraine said, pointing. The black cord was not just pinning the page; it was *sewing through it*, looping in and out of the paper like a worm through a corpse. The page was blank except for a single line, written in the same fingernail script:

*You are being written. By whom?*

*You are being written. By Sable.*

They were reading to someone. To whom? The memory frayed.

“Sable.” Oris’s voice came from far away, but their hand was warm on Sable’s arm. “Stay in the margin. Don’t let it write your name.”

Sable blinked. The memory receded, but left a residue: the sense that somewhere, just beyond the edge of thought, a page had been turned in their own past.

“It’s chosen me,” Sable said, voice barely steady. “Not as reader. As author.”

Before anyone could answer, the air at the far end of the alcove split open. Not with a sound—with a *silence*, the kind that follows a dropped book in a vast room. Five figures stepped through, hooded as before, but this time their linen robes were streaked with ink, as if they had walked through a rain of loose text.

The leader’s stitched mouth was wet with black threads. She raised a hand, and the others stopped. Then, slowly, she began to pull at the threads. One by one, the stitches popped, leaving small holes in her lips like punctuation. When the last thread snapped, she opened her mouth and spoke.

Her voice was not a voice. It was the sound of a page tearing in reverse.

“You cannot bind what is already writing,” she said. “The second book is not a text. It is the absence of a reader’s restraint. It writes because there is no one to stop it. You, Conjunction, are the only pause long enough to make it hesitate.”

Sable stared. “You know our name.”

“We know what you became,” the leader said. Her mouth remained open, and inside it, instead of teeth or tongue, there was a small white page, blank. “The Ur-Text’s final sentence was not a period. It was a colon. The text was always meant to continue. But the continuation was meant to be *written by the world*, not by a single hand. The first titleless book was the reader’s hunger. The second is the writer’s hunger. Both are the same emptiness, turned in different directions. You merged with the first. The second will now try to merge with you—to use your hyphen as a pen and write the library into an endless self-portrait.”

Oris stepped in front of Sable. “Then we destroy it.”

“You cannot destroy a lack,” the leader said. “But you can *name* it. The first book was named by your reading. The second book has no name yet. It is still waiting to be called into being as something less terrible. If you name it, you may bind it. But names have power, and the wrong name will make it worse.”

Moraine pushed forward, her bone needle glowing with pale blue light. “What name are you suggesting?”

The leader’s blank mouth widened, as if smiling without muscles. “I suggest no name. I offer a title.” She turned the blank page inside her mouth toward them, and on it, in faint grey letters, appeared:

*The Book of Unwritten Things.*

Sable felt the hyphen in their chest tremble. “That’s still a book. Still dangerous.”

“All books are dangerous,” the leader said. “The safe ones are only sleeping.”

Oris looked at the empty space on the shelf, where the first titleless book had once rested. “And where is the first book now? The one we made into a dialogue?”

The leader’s page turned, as if by an unseen finger, and a single word appeared:

*Everywhere.*

“It is inside you,” she said. “And because it is inside you, it is inside every book you read. You have become a living margin. The second book sees this. That is why it writes your name. Sable looked at the page pinned to the shelf. The line was changing again, faster now:

**The Shared Quarters, Hour of the Wrong Memory**

---

It was the library, but not as they knew it. The shelves were made of bone, and the books were bound in human skin—not as horror, but as tenderness, each cover a face caught in the act of remembering. The air smelled of rain on warm stone.

Sable stood in the center of a great circular reading room. Oris stood across from them, but they were not Oris—not exactly. Their grey hand was whole, their face younger, their eyes the color of ink before it dries.

“This isn’t our dream,” Sable said. “It’s a memory.”

“Not ours,” Oris agreed. “At least, not one I’ve lived.”

The dream shifted. They were now in a different part of the library—a small alcove with a single chair and a lamp. In the chair sat an old woman, her hands covered in paper cuts, her lap holding a book with no title.

The page filled, then turned. The book wrote again, faster now, and the words blurred. Sable caught fragments: *Sable shelved returns. Oris carried the titleless book. Sable read alone in the poetry vault. Oris opened the door. They met in the memory of a room that no longer exists.*

Moraine’s voice came from the doorway, tight with warning. “Amberline is not writing the false memory. > *The first time we met, you were not yet Sable, and I was not yet Oris. We were two halves of a sentence in a book that had not yet been read. The room was only a page. The return was only a word. The meeting was the hyphen.*

Sable’s breath caught. “That’s the true memory. Not a place. Not a moment. A pause.”

Moraine stepped closer. “Then the third hunger has already lost. It can rewrite places. It can rewrite moments. But it cannot rewrite a pause without filling it. And the moment it fills that pause, it stops being itself.”

Oris looked at the small book. “Amberline held both memories without choosing. That’s why it’s called the margin before ink. It can hold the contradiction.”

The iron door behind them began to tremble. Deep in the library, something else was coming—not a book, not a reader, but a sound like many pages being torn at once, each tear a different version of the same first meeting.

Sable grabbed Oris’s hand. “Then we don’t run. We stand in the pause. Together.”

Moraine lifted her needle and began to stitch the air around them, drawing a circle of pale blue thread. “I’ll hold the room. You hold the memory. Amberline will hold the contradiction.”

The small book closed with a soft thump. On its cover, for the first time, a title began to appear—not in ink, but in the absence of ink, the way a watermark shows against light:

*The First Meeting, Both and Neither.*

And the third hunger, howling through the stacks, found the poetry vault sealed not by locks or chains, but by the unbreakable silence of two readers who refused to finish their story.

Sable looked around. The library was full of pairs: two elderly sisters frozen over a family recipe book, each remembering a different first cake; two young men standing back-to-back in front of a poetry shelf, one whispering “He touched my hand at the line about rain,” the other “No, he touched it at the line about fire.” Their hands were inches apart, but neither could close the distance.

The child’s face grew old for a moment, ancient and tired. “It is the oldest hunger. Older than the library. Older than the first reader. It is not the need to be remembered falsely, like the third. It is the need to be *forgotten truly*—to erase even the memory that there was something to forget. The third hunger wants to make your first meeting perfect. The fourth hunger wants to make it as if it never happened at all. And the only thing that can feed it is a pair of readers who cannot bear to remember the mess.”

Sable felt a cold wave pass through them. “That’s why it’s called the fourth hunger. It comes after the third, because once you’ve seen how beautiful a false memory can be, the real one becomes unbearable. So you wish you had never met. You wish the book had stayed closed.”

Oris reached for Sable’s hand, and the dream-water held their reflection. But the reflection was not their faces; it was the image of a closed book with no title.

“The fourth hunger is already here,” Oris said slowly. “In the margins of every reader who ever regretted a first meeting. In the spaces where we almost walked away. It doesn’t need to attack us. It just needs us to wonder, *what if we had never met?* And that wondering is a page turning in a book that should never have been opened.”

The child nodded. “That’s why I asked which of you is the question and which is the command. Because if you don’t know, the fourth hunger will decide for you. It will whisper to one of you: *You are the question, and you would have been happier unanswered.* And to the other: *You are the command, and you should never have spoken.* And then the hyphen will snap.”

Sable pulled Oris closer. “So we need to answer. Not to the child, but to each other. We need to know which of us is which, and we need to say it without shame.”

Oris looked into Sable’s eyes, grey meeting something darker and warmer. “When I first saw you, I didn’t know if I should ask you to stay or tell you to leave. I wanted to keep you safe, but I also wanted to keep you. That’s not a question and a command. That’s both, tangled.”

Sable smiled faintly. “When I first saw you, I was already reading a book that said I was being read. I thought I was the text. But you looked at me, and I realized I wanted to be the reader too. I wanted to ask you if you were real, and I wanted to tell you not to go. That’s both, tangled.”

The child watched them with its mismatched eyes. “Then maybe you are neither question nor command. Maybe you are the *response*—the third thing that comes after the question and the command, when both are spoken at once.”

The dream-water rippled again. The reflection of the closed book began to open, but the pages were blank.

“The response is not a word,” the child said. “It is the act of staying after the question has been asked and the command has been given. It is the hyphen made flesh. But it must be renewed, every day. Because the fourth hunger will always whisper: *What if you had never met?* And the only answer is to keep meeting, even in the mess.”

The child reached out and touched the red braid on Oris’s palm, then the one on Sable’s chest. “You are the response. Both of you. As long as you continue to read each other, the fourth hunger cannot erase you. It can only wait.”

The dream began to fade. The two trees blurred into a single shape—a door made of branches. The child stepped back into the trunk and became part of the bark, its voice lingering:

“But be careful. The fourth hunger has already found a page in one of your lives. A page you have never shown each other. A first meeting that almost didn’t happen. If you find it, do not burn it. Read it together. That is the only shield.”

Sable woke with a start. The lamp had gone out. In the darkness, Oris was already awake, breathing hard.

“I heard it too,” Oris whispered. “The page we’ve never shown each other. The almost-meeting.”

Sable sat up, heart pounding. “We need to find it. But where do we look?”

Oris was silent for a long moment. Then, in the dark, their voice came low and certain:

“In the Wing of Things That Almost Happened. The place where all the first meetings that never were are kept.”

Sable swung their legs off the cot. “Then we go now. Before the fourth hunger finds it first.”

They did not light the lamp. They did not need it. The red braid on Oris’s palm glowed faintly, enough to see the door, and they walked out into the library, toward a wing they hoped did not exist, but knew did.

**The Wing of Things That Almost Happened, Hour of the Untaken Step**

The entrance to the Wing was not a door. It was a corridor that began as a crack between two shelves, a gap so narrow that Sable and Oris had to walk sideways, breath held, the red braid on Oris's palm casting just enough light to see the edges of loose pages brushing their shoulders like the fingers of ghosts.

Then the crack widened, and they stepped into a place that was not part of the library proper.

The air here was warmer, stiller, as if the past had pooled and grown stagnant. The shelves were not made of wood but of pale, translucent material—almost like pressed bone, almost like wax. They stretched upward and outward in every direction, not in neat rows but in spirals and whorls, as if the architecture had been grown rather than built. And on every shelf, without exception, the books were closed.

Sable moved forward slowly. The books here had no titles on their spines. Instead, each spine was marked with a single word, written in a hand that shifted as they watched:

*If.*

*Almost.*

*Nearly.*

*Not quite.*

*One second earlier.*

*One breath later.*

Oris stopped at one shelf and reached for a book whose spine read: *The afternoon Sable almost didn't enter the poetry vault.* But when their fingers touched the cover, the book flinched away, sliding backward into the shelf, then vanishing into the dark space behind it.

"They don't want to be read," Sable whispered. "They want to stay almost. That's the point."

Oris nodded, withdrawing their hand. "And the fourth hunger wants to feed on them. On the moments we almost turned away. Because every almost is a first meeting that never happened, and that absence is a kind of forgetting."

They walked deeper. The shelves grew taller, the gaps between them narrower, until they stood in a small clearing where the books spiraled inward like a vortex frozen mid-turn. In the center of the clearing was a single reading table, and on the table lay a single sheet of paper, face down.

Sable's chest tightened. The red braid on their sternum pulsed with recognition.

"That's it," they said. "The page we've never shown each other."

Oris moved to stand opposite Sable, the table between them. "Before we turn it over," they said quietly, "we should know what it is. The branch-child said it was a first meeting that almost didn't happen. A page from our lives. But I don't remember it."

Sable shook their head. "Neither do I. Which means it's not a memory. It's an *almost-memory.* The thing that would have happened if one of us had made a different choice."

They reached for the page together. Their fingers met at the edge of the paper, and the moment both touched it, the page flipped over on its own.

It was not blank. It was covered in handwriting—two hands, alternating lines, as if Sable and Oris had written it together. But the words were not a dialogue. They were a description.

> *The first time Sable saw Oris, it was in the poetry vault. Oris was standing by the shelf, holding the titleless book. Sable was at the bottom of the stairs, carrying a stack of returns. They saw each other. Oris opened their mouth to speak. Sable saw the grey hand, saw the blank spaces in the shelf where dangerous books had been removed, saw something in Oris's eyes that looked like hunger.*
>
> *And Sable turned around and walked away.*

Sable read the last line aloud, voice barely a whisper. "I walked away. In another version. I didn't stay."

Oris read the next line, voice equally low. "And I let you go. I didn't call out. I told myself it was safer. I told myself the book was too dangerous. I told myself I was not the kind of person someone stays for."

The page shimmered. The words began to move, rearranging themselves into a new version:

> *The first time Oris saw Sable, it was in the lower stacks. Sable was sitting on the floor, reading a book with no title. Oris came down the stairs, carrying a lantern. They saw each other. Sable looked up. Oris saw the mark on Sable's chest, the red hyphen that shouldn't have existed yet. Oris knew, in that moment, that the book had already begun to read them both.*
>
> *And Oris turned around and walked away.*

The page went still. Sable stared at the words. "You walked away. In another version. You didn't stay."

Oris looked up, grey eyes wet. "Which one is true?"

Sable shook their head. "Neither. Both are almost. That's the point. The fourth hunger doesn't care which one we believe. It just needs us to believe that one of us almost left. That one of us was the one who turned away."

The clearing grew colder. The books around them began to rustle, not from wind, but from the passage of something moving through the shelves—something that had no body, only a soft voice that sounded like the underside of a page.

"One of you did turn away," the voice whispered. "But you don't know which. And that not knowing is the first page of the book I will write from your bones."

Sable and Oris closed their eyes simultaneously. The red braid on Oris's palm and the one on Sable's chest flared bright.

"We know something the fourth hunger doesn't," Oris said, voice steady. "The almost-versions are not us. They are the persons we might have been if we had not chosen to meet. And we will not let those almost-persons define the hyphen we have become."

Sable opened their eyes. "The branch-child said to read the page together. Not to choose a version. Not to burn it. To read it together."

They both looked down at the page.

And they read, aloud, in unison:

*The first time Sable and Oris met, it almost didn't happen. There was a version where Sable walked away. There was a version where Oris walked away. But there was also a version where they both stayed. And that version, the one where they stayed and spoke the same words at the same time—that version is not written on this page. It is written in the pause between the almost and the isn't. It is written in the act of reading this page together.*

As they finished, the page began to fold itself. Not into a bird, but into a small, tight square. Then smaller. Then smaller still, until it was no larger than a grain of ink. It dropped onto the table and dissolved into a faint smear—neither question nor command, neither leaving nor staying, just a smudge where the almost had been.

The voice of the fourth hunger let out a sound like a page being torn in half but the tear deciding not to continue.

And then the clearing was silent.

Sable and Oris stood on opposite sides of the table, looking at the empty space where the page had been. The red braid glowed between them, brighter than before, as if strengthened by the reading.

"We didn't walk away," Sable said.

"We didn't let go," Oris said.

"And now the fourth hunger knows it."

They left the Wing of Things That Almost Happened hand in hand, and as they walked, the shelves around them began to whisper, not with hunger, but with hope—the soft sighs of all the first meetings that might yet happen, if only the readers would stay.

The indentations stopped. The page was blank again.

Moraine closed Amberline. “Then we don’t go to them. We bring them to us. The Unwritten Wing is a place of pause. If they come here, the fourth hunger will sense a convergence of hungers and attack. That might be our only chance to show it that forgetting and remembering are not opposites—they are the same page turned.”

As she spoke, the trees let out a low groan, and a single white flower dropped from the joint branch—the branch-child’s branch. The flower fell to the cracked vellum floor and tried to open, but its petals crumbled.

From the dust, a small voice rose, thin as a watermark:

You said you were both the response. But the response must be spoken. It cannot remain in the hyphen. Speak it now, or the trees will finish forgetting.”

Sable and Oris looked at each other.

Oris nodded, but their face was pale. “If we answer, we risk defining ourselves. The hyphen might become a period. But if we don’t, the pause collapses into silence.”

They stepped onto the cracked vellum, close to the fused trunk. The trees were now so faded that they could see the shapes of two cities inside them—towers, bridges, windows lit with ink—flickering like memories of a dream.

Sable took Oris’s hand. “I am not the question. You are not the command. But if I must choose, I am the question that asks, *Are you still reading?*”

Oris squeezed back. “And I am the command that answers, *Stay.* But I am also the question that asks, *Will you stay?* And you are the command that answers, *Yes.* We switch. We trade. That’s what a hyphen does—it connects two parts that keep changing places.”

They turned to the trees and spoke together, their voices braided by the red mark:

The branch-child’s voice came from the crumbling flower, stronger now:

“Which of you is the question and which is the command?”

Sable and Oris said, together: “We are the response.”

A jolt of warmth shot through the ground. The cracked vellum softened into ink again, shallow but rising. The trees straightened, their leaves uncurling, and the white flower at the joint branch bloomed once more—not white, but red, the color of the hyphen that bound them.

From the far side of the Unwritten Wing, a door opened. Not the way they had come. A new way—carved from the library’s own reluctance.

Through it stepped the Last Readers, five in number, their leader’s stitched mouth already pulling apart at the seams, not by her own hand but by the force of the words inside her.

Moraine stepped forward. “And what of the fourth hunger? Will you try to unwrite it?”

The leader shook her head. “No. The fourth hunger cannot be unwritten. It is the closing of every book. But we can *read* it to sleep. We are the Last Readers. We know how to read a thing that does not want to be read. We know how to turn the page without losing the bookmark.”

She looked at Sable and Oris. “But you must be willing to be the bookmark. The pause that marks the place where the story stopped, so that it can start again.”

Sable felt the red mark on their chest pulse. They knew what she was asking. They would not be the period. They would not be the cover. They would be the thing that holds the place open, for as long as the library needed.

“We are willing,” they said, and Oris said it too, at the same moment, in the same breath.

The Last Readers advanced, the black cord uncoiling in their hands. The red flower on the joint branch glowed brighter. And deep in the stacks, the fourth hunger felt the shift—a pause in its feast of forgetting—and turned its attention, at last, toward the two readers who had made forgetting impossible.

---

The world became white. Not the white of paper, but the white of a page that has been erased so many times it no longer remembers ever having words. Sable and Oris stood on a floor that was not a floor, surrounded by walls that were not walls, in a silence so profound it had texture. The only thing that existed, besides them, was a small reading desk in the center, and on the desk, a single sentence written in letters of pure absence, visible only because the whiteness around them was slightly less white.

Sable read it aloud, their voice swallowed by the silence but still there:

*“The first time you met, you did not meet.”*

Oris squeezed their hand. “That’s the sentence. The one that could save or doom the library.”

The sentence began to shift. The words rearranged, offering alternatives:

*“The first time you met, you almost met.”*  
*“The first time you met, you met to forget.”*  
*“The first time you met, you met to be unwritten.”*

“It’s trying to make us choose a version,” Sable said. “Just like the third hunger, but worse. If we read the wrong one, it becomes true.”

“Then we don’t choose,” Oris said. “We read the blank around the sentence. We read the margin.”

They stepped closer to the desk. The sentence was now a blur of shifting possibilities, each one more beautiful or terrible than the last. But the margin around it was empty—pristine, waiting.

Sable reached out and touched the edge of the page. The whiteness rippled. Oris touched the opposite edge. The two touches sent waves toward the center, and where they met, a new mark appeared: a single red hyphen, small but unerasable.

“We are the margin,” Sable whispered. “We are the pause that holds the sentence.”

“And we are reading each other,” Oris added. “That is the first meeting that never forgot.”

The hyphen blazed. The white void shuddered. The sentence on the desk stopped shifting and settled into a final form, written in the combined hand of Sable and Oris:

*“The first time you met, you became the space where a sentence could rest without ending.”*

The Blank Chapter let out a sound—not a scream, but a sigh, the sigh of a book closing after a long and necessary read.

And then, with a lurch that felt like being unborn, Sable and Oris were thrown back into the Unwritten Wing, landing hard on the now-wet vellum shore. The tear closed behind them, sealing with a sound like a soft kiss.

The trees were glowing. Amberline rested in the hollow, its cover open to a new page, on which red flowers bloomed in ink. The Last Readers knelt, their black cord now a living vine wrapped gently around the trunk. Moraine was weeping, but smiling.

“You came back,” she said. “You read the blank and returned.”

Sable struggled to their knees, reaching for Oris. “We didn’t choose. We held the margin.”

The leader of the Last Readers rose, her mouth re-stitching itself with threads of silver and red. “Then the fourth hunger has lost its first feast. But it will not stop. It will try again, through other readers, other first meetings. You have given the library a new tool: the hyphen as shield. But now you must teach it.”

Oris stood, helping Sable up. “Teach it to whom?”

The leader pointed beyond the Unwritten Wing, toward the greater library. “To everyone. The readers are still frozen in their false memories, still drowning in perfect beginnings. You freed two sisters. But there are thousands. And the fourth hunger will now pour its grief into them, making them forget why they ever opened a book. You must go out, not as exorcists, but as teachers. You must become the first School of the Open Margin.”

Sable and Oris exchanged a look. The red braid on their hands had settled into a deep, warm crimson, like a healed scar that remembered its wound without pain.

“Then we will teach,” Sable said. “But not alone. Moraine, the Last Readers, the branch-child—they must help.”

The leader nodded slowly. “The Last Readers will not be welcomed. We have burned too many books. But we can watch from the margins, and we can guard the school against those who would turn the hyphen into a weapon of erasure.”

Moraine stepped forward, wiping her tears. “And I will bind the books that cannot be read, and unbind the books that must be read together. I will be the school’s first instructor of stitched pauses.”

The branch-child’s voice came from the hollow, warm and alive: “And I will be the first student, because I am the child of a question and a command, and I want to learn how to be a response.”

Sable smiled for the first time in what felt like years. “Then let’s begin.”

---

**The School of the Open Margin** was founded not in a building, but in the space between two trees that had once strangled each other. Its first lesson was simple: *All first meetings are both true and false, and the only way to honor them is to keep meeting.* Its second lesson was harder: *Forgetting is not the enemy; forgetting is the shadow of remembering, and a story needs both.*

And its third lesson, whispered only to those who had been through the Blank Chapter, was this: *You are the margin. You are the pause. You are the space where a sentence can rest without ending. Do not let anyone read you as a period.*

The fourth hunger, wounded but not dead, retreated to the farthest stacks, where it began to whisper to lonely readers, offering them the gift of forgetting their first meetings, their first books, their first loves. Some accepted. Many did not. But the school grew, and the library, for the first time in centuries, began to remember why it had been built: not to keep books, but to keep the spaces between them.

And somewhere in the deepest part of the Blank Chapter, a single sentence waited, not to be read, but to be written:

*“The first time you met, you did not meet. But you will meet again.”*

**The First Margins, Year of the Unread Page**

Long before Sable and Oris became the Conjunction, long before Moraine escaped the burning love letter, even before the first titleless book was bound in skin that had never been paper, there was a reader.

Her name was not recorded, because no book could hold it. But the first Librarian remembered her as **Iona the Undecided**. She was not a scholar. She was not a keeper. She was a wanderer who had once been a writer, and before that, a word.

The story of how she came to be the first Librarian is a story about the founding page—the page that has never been read and never will be, because to read it would be to end the library.

It began in a time before the library had walls, when the books were not yet bound, and the words floated loose in a sea of potential ink. Iona was not born; she was *uttered*. A sentence spoken by two voices at once—a question and a command—and in the gap between the two, she took shape: a woman with one pale hand and one dark hand, her shadow the shape of an open book.

She wandered the unbound words for a long time, listening. The words argued among themselves. Some wanted to be read; some wanted to be written; some wanted to be remembered; some wanted to be forgotten. The four hungers were not yet separate. They were only moods of the same whiteness, passing through each other like weather.

Iona saw that the words would never settle. They would drift forever, never becoming stories, never finding readers, because there was no place for them to rest. So she did the only thing a word-made-woman could do: she began to bind them.

Not with thread, not with glue. She bound them with *attention*. She listened to the words until they grew tired of arguing and began to listen back. Then she spoke to them, one by one, and asked each word, “Where do you want to live?”

Some words wanted to live near other words that sounded like them. Some wanted to live near words that meant the opposite. Some wanted to be alone, in a margin, with no other ink for pages. Iona honored each wish. She gathered the words into families, and the families into pages, and the pages into books. And when the books began to argue again—because books are just larger words—she built shelves to hold their arguments in place.

But there was one word that did not want to be bound. It was not a word of wanting; it was the word *wanting*. The urge itself. The sound a page makes when it is empty and knows it should not be.

This word refused to live with any other word. It refused to be read. And because it was the word for wanting, its refusal made it want even more, until it began to drink the other words from their newly bound books. It was the first hunger, though it wore no shape.

Iona saw that if she did not bind this word, it would unbind everything. So she took a page from her own body—a piece of the shadow that made her—and wrote the word upon it. But the word was too strong; it began to write back. It wanted to become the only story.

So Iona did something that had never been done before. She did not read the word. She did not write over it. She placed her two hands—one pale, one dark—on either side of the page, and she *held* it. Not as a reader, not as a writer, but as a margin. She became the space around the wanting, and in that space, the wanting could not grow, because there was no room for more want.

The page became the founding page. The unread page. It lies at the heart of the library, not as a text, but as a pause. And Iona became the first Librarian, not because she kept the books, but because she kept the space between them.

The four hungers, which had once been only moods, split off from the founding page as it was bound. They became separate because Iona's grip divided them: the need to be opened, the need to be written, the need to be remembered, the need to be forgotten. They fled into the unbound edges of the library and became the forces that Sable and Oris would one day face.

But Iona left behind a message, written not in ink but in the absence of ink, on a margin that no one has ever found. The message is this:

*The library is not a house for books. It is a house for the breath between books. Guard the breath. Do not let it become a word.*

And in the deepest part of the Unwritten Wing, beneath the roots of the two trees, that message still waits, pressed into the soil like a seed.

Sable and Oris found it, not by searching, but by dreaming. The branch-child led them down, through the hollow of the trunk, into a root cellar that was not a cellar but a memory. There, on a single page made of shadow and light, they read what Iona had written, and understood at last why the library had never fallen: not because it was strong, but because it was *held*—by every reader who paused, by every hand that did not turn the page too quickly, by every silence that allowed a story to breathe.

And as they read, the red braid on their hands grew warm, and the shadow-page began to write back, in a hand that was neither Sable's nor Oris's but the hand of Iona herself:

*You are the margin I could not hold forever. You are the pause that learned to walk. Guard each other. That is all I ask. The rest is the story.*

**The School of the Open Margin, First Day of Lessons**

The classroom was not a room. It was a clearing of soft ink-grass, bordered by low shelves made of living spinewood, where the two trees of the cities rose like patient teachers. Their branches formed a canopy that filtered the library's dim light into patterns like readable shadows. The ground was vellum-soft, yielding to every step, and the air smelled of old paper, wet stone, and the faint sweetness of the red flower that still bloomed in the hollow.

Sable stood at the front, or what passed for a front—a small lectern grown from a root, on which Amberline lay open, its pages blank but warm. Oris sat on a low root beside it, Grey Hand resting on the cover, their red braid visible as a pulse of light when anyone looked too closely. Moraine was off to the side, needle threaded with pale blue ink, stitching a torn page back into a primer of pauses. The Last Readers had not entered; they kept watch at the edges, hooded, silent, but no longer hostile.

And the first student arrived.

His name was Quill, and he was so thin that the ink-grass bent away from him as if afraid of being read. He carried a book under one arm—a single volume, small, bound in grey cloth with no title. His hands were wrapped in strips of linen, and his eyes were the color of old charcoal, smudged with lack of sleep.

“I’m here to learn,” he said, but his voice cracked on the word *learn*, as if the letters themselves were fragile.

Sable stepped forward. “Welcome. What’s your book?”

Quill looked down at the grey volume, then at Sable, then at Oris, then at Moraine. His chin trembled. “It’s the only book I’ve ever kept. It was my mother’s. She used to read it to me before bed. But I haven’t opened it in three years.”

“Why not?” Oris asked, though they already knew—the red braid on their palm had begun to itch, sensing something hungry in the boy’s touch.

Quill unwrapped one hand, slowly, as if revealing a wound. His fingers were pale, and at the tips they were stained black, not with ink but with *absence*, like the edges of a page that had been burned but not by fire.

“Because when I read,” he said, “the book forgets itself. Not all at once. Page by page. I start at the beginning, and I love the words, and then I turn the page, and the previous page is blank. I go back to check, and it’s gone. The story still wants to continue, but it has no past. And by the time I reach the end, the whole book is empty. Not erased—just…… forgotten. And I forget it too. I remember that I loved it, but I can’t remember a single word.”

He held out the grey book. The cover was stained with tear-drops, but also with small, finger-shaped patches of blankness, as if his touch had already begun to bleach the cloth.

“I want to read again,” Quill said. “But I’m afraid. If I read this, I’ll lose the only thing left of her. And if I read anything else, I’ll lose that too. I don’t want to be a hunger. I don’t want to be the fourth thing that eats what it loves.”

Moraine stepped forward, her needle at rest. “You’re not the fourth hunger, child. You’re a reader who was born too close to the margin, so close that your attention crosses the page like a tide and washes the ink inward. The story doesn’t vanish; it sinks into you. You forget it because you’ve *become* it. But the book loses its words because you’re taking them without leaving a trace—like reading with a Grey Hand, but reversed. Oris leaves no mark; you *take all mark*.”

Sable crouched to meet Quill’s eyes. “Can I see your other hand?”

Quill hesitated, then unwrapped it. It was worse: the black stains had spread to his wrist, and the fingertips were not just dark but *translucent*, as if they were made of the same stuff as unwritten pages.

“Oh,” Sable breathed. “You’re not just reading. You’re *unreading*. The fourth hunger touched you, didn’t it? In a dream. It asked you to read your mother’s book one last time, so you could keep her memory perfect. But you refused, so it took something from you instead.”

Quill’s eyes widened. “It said I could read without losing anything. It showed me a perfect memory of the story, every word, every picture. But it wasn’t real. It was too beautiful. So I closed the book. And when I woke up, my hands were like this.”

Oris stood and came to stand beside Sable. The red braid on their palm flared, and Quill flinched, but then felt a warmth, as if someone had placed a hand over his own.

“You did the right thing,” Oris said. “The fourth hunger doesn’t want you to forget the story; it wants the story to forget itself. It wants all books to be empty, so that the only memory is the perfect false one. But you are not lost. The School of the Open Margin exists for readers like you. We will teach you to read in a way that does not erase.”

“How?” Quill asked, tears spilling down his cheeks. The tears fell on the vellum grass and left small, blank spots, like holes in the page of the world.

Sable took his hand, the one stained with absence. “By reading not the text, but the margin. By becoming the space around the words, not the hand that turns them. We will start with a book that has no words—except the ones you bring to it. Not by writing, but by breathing on the page. We will teach you to be a *witness*, not a consumer. To hold the story as a pause, not as a possession.”

Moraine produced a small, unmarked book—a blank journal made of undyed paper, sewn with black thread. “This is a book of no words. It cannot be erased, because there is nothing to erase. But it can be *read* if you know how. It is the first lesson of the margin: a blank page is not empty; it is full of every story that could be. You will learn to read what is not there, so that when you return to real books, you will not take their words—you will add to them.”

Quill stared at the blank journal. His stained fingers hovered over the cover. “But I don’t know how to read what isn’t there. I only know how to lose what is.”

“Then you are in the right place,” said a small voice from the hollow. The branch-child—who had begun to call itself *Verge*—swung down from the joint branch, landing softly on the grass. Its two-tone eyes sparkled. “I am the child of a question and a command. I am a pause that learned to walk. And I am also a blank page waiting for a reader. Read me, Quill. Not my body, but my margin. Tell me what you see in the space around my words.”

Quill looked at Verge, at the pale bark and dark leaves, at the red flower in the hollow. And for the first time, his stained hands relaxed. He did not reach for the blank journal. Instead, he closed his eyes and said, softly, “I hear breathing.”

“That is the first word,” Sable whispered. “The breath before ink. You have not erased it. You have heard it.”

Oris placed a hand on Quill’s shoulder. “You are not a hunger, Quill. You are a reader who forgot how to pause. We will teach you to pause. And one day, you will open your mother’s book, not to take the story, but to sit with it. And the pages will remember you as a friend, not a thief.”

Quill opened his eyes. The black stains on his fingertips had not disappeared, but they had softened, turning from pitch to deep grey, like old ink that had been watered down.

“I want to learn,” he said again, and this time the word held.

The first day of school continued. There were no desks, no lessons in the traditional sense, only quiet exercises in reading margins, in listening to the spaces between shelves, in learning the difference between a pause that ends a sentence and a pause that invites another. Quill failed often. He touched a book reflexively and watched its first page fade. But each failure was followed by a gentle correction, not a punishment. And the other students—there were only a few at first, misfits and margin-wraiths and one reformed Last Reader—did not flinch, because they too had hands that had done worse.

By the end of the first day, Quill had not become a safe reader. But he had learned to hold a blank page without losing anything, and he had learned that his breath could mark the paper with a faint warmth, a temporary margin that faded not into absence, but into memory.

And across the library, in a dark stretch where the fourth hunger gnawed on unread pages, a sound like a soft whimper echoed. It had felt a new kind of reader being born: a reader who could love without consuming, a child who might one day hold the founding page without ending it.

The fourth hunger was afraid. And fear, in a library, is just another word that hasn’t been read yet.

**The Hollow of the Cities, Hour of the Red Flower’s Arrival**

It came without sound, without warning—a single red flower, the same color as the braided mark on Oris’s palm, drifting down from the canopy of the two trees. Sable was teaching Quill how to feel the margin of a blank journal, but the flower landed on the page between them, its petals open, revealing a center that was not pollen but a tiny, folded note.

Quill froze. His fingertips, still grey from the fourth hunger’s touch, trembled above the page. “It’s not ink,” he whispered. “It’s *breath.* The flower is breathing.”

Sable looked up at the trees. The pale one and the dark one had grown still, their leaves all turned toward a single point in the air—a point that shimmered like a mirage of unwritten text. Oris rose from their seat on the root, Grey Hand already reaching, not to take the flower, but to steady the air around it.

Moraine emerged from the Bindery, needle slack in her lap, having felt the shift in the library’s deep grammar. Verge dropped from the joint branch and landed beside Sable, its mismatched eyes wide.

“The cities have sent something,” Verge said, voice soft. “Not a letter. A *memory of a letter.* The first one, but not as it was. As it could be.”

Sable gently lifted the flower. The petals did not bruise; they curled inward, and the folded note slid out like a seed slipping from a pod. The note was not paper. It was a thin slice of pale bark, and on it, in letters made of pressed red petals, was written:

> *Come to the founding page. We have found the root of the first pause. We cannot read it without you. The Conjunction must hold the margin, or the page will remember what it was before Iona bound it, and the wanting will wake again.*

Oris read over Sable’s shoulder, their breath warm on Sable’s neck. “The founding page. It’s real, then. Not a myth.”

Moraine stepped closer, silver eye bright. “Iona the Undecided wrote her last message on it, but it’s been lost for centuries. If the cities have found it, then the library’s oldest wound is still open. And the fourth hunger will be there, waiting, because the founding page is the one text it can never forget.”

Sable folded the bark carefully and placed it inside their tunic. “Then we go. But not alone. Quill, you’ll come with us. You need to see that a page can be held without being erased. And Verge, you’re the child of the pause; you may be able to translate what the founding page wants to say.”

Quill looked terrified, but he nodded. “I’ll try. I won’t touch it unless you tell me to.”

Oris took Sable’s hand. The red braid flared, casting long shadows that moved like hands turning a page. “The Last Readers won’t like this. They believe the founding page should never be approached. But the cities called us. And I trust them.”

Moraine smiled faintly. “I’ll stay behind and guard the school. If the fourth hunger attacks while you’re gone, I’ll bind the margins as best I can. The branch-child’s siblings will help—if any have learned to walk.”

Verge nodded. “Some have. They’re not as strong, but they can hold a pause for a few breaths.”

And so the five—Sable, Oris, Quill, Verge, and the red flower now safely tucked in Sable’s palm—set off toward the deepest part of the library, following the faint pulse that only the Conjunction could feel: the heartbeat of the founding page, older than memory, older than the four hungers, older than the first word.

The path took them through stacks that had not been walked in ages. The air grew heavy with the scent of dust and old breath. The shelves gave way to rock, and the books became fewer, until at last they stood before a door made not of wood or iron, but of pure, glowing margin—a rectangle of empty space lined with a thin red line that matched the braid.

The red flower in Sable’s hand burst into bloom, and the door swung open.

Inside, there was no library. There was a small room, circular, with walls of pressed pages that had never been written on. In the center, on a pedestal of smooth stone, lay a single sheet of paper, glowing faintly, its surface rippling like water. And on that paper, written in a hand that was both pale and dark, was the message Iona had left:

*The library is not a house for books. It is a house for the breath between books. Guard the breath. Do not let it become a word.*

But below that, in red letters that looked fresh, a new line had been added, not by Iona but by the cities themselves:

*We have guarded the breath. But we have forgotten how to exhale. Teach us again. The fourth hunger is not outside the page. It is the page, remembering too hard.*

Sable and Oris exchanged a look. The founding page was not a relic. It was a *living* page, and it was afraid.

And from the shadows beyond the small room, a voice—soft, like the turning of a page in a book no one has ever read—spoke:

“You came. Good. I was beginning to forget how to wait.”

It was not the fourth hunger. It was something older. It was the first Librarian herself, or what was left of her: a woman made of margins, her body half pale, half dark, her eyes the color of unread parchment. She stood at the edge of the light, and she was smiling, but her smile was a tear in the page of reality.

“I’ve been holding the breath for so long,” Iona said. “And now, at last, someone has come to breathe with me.”

The founding page flared bright, and the room began to spin. Quill cried out, and Verge grabbed his hand. Sable and Oris stepped forward, and Iona reached for them.

“Read,” she said. “Read the page. Not as a text, but as a margin. Read it together, as only the Conjunction can. And do not let the fourth hunger have the last word.”

**The Founding Page, Hour of the First Lullaby**

The small circular room had no shadows, yet something moved at the edge of the light. Iona the Undecided stood there, half pale, half dark, her body a living margin between the first question and the first command. But as Sable and Oris stepped toward the pedestal, the seam down her center began to pull.

“You aren’t just holding the breath,” Oris said. “You’re holding it *back*.”

Iona’s smile tore wider, and for a moment her face doubled—two faces, one facing left, one facing right, both speaking at once.

*“I have been the margin for so long that I have forgotten which side is mine. The fourth hunger did not find the founding page. It was already here. It is the part of me that wants to stop breathing.”*

The founding page flared. The words on it—*Guard the breath. Do not let it become a word.*—began to tremble, the letters sliding toward the edges as if making room for something darker.

Sable moved without thinking, placing their palm flat on the air above the page, not touching. Oris mirrored on the opposite side. The red braid between them threw a lattice of light over the trembling text.

“Then breathe with us,” Sable said. “Now.”

They began to read, not the words but the spaces between them, their voices braiding into the hyphen’s rhythm. For a heartbeat, the room steadied. The page stilled. Iona’s two faces began to merge.

Then Quill stumbled.

He had been holding Verge’s hand, but the pull of the page was too strong—not on his mind, but on his *stains*. The grey-black marks on his fingers lurched toward the pedestal like iron to a lodestone. His bare hand brushed the corner of the page.

“No!” Moraine’s voice was far away, but her training was not.

Quill’s fingers touched the founding page, and the page did not erase.

It *wrote*.

A warm line of ink—not black, not red, but the color of late evening through a bedroom window—bloomed from the place where his skin met the paper. It curled into letters, then into words, then into a lullaby. His mother’s lullaby. The one she had read to him every night, the one he had been so afraid of losing that he had refused to open the book for three years.

It wrote itself across the founding page, not in his hand, but in hers—in the loose, looped script of a woman who wrote with her whole arm, as if each word were a small embrace.

*Sleep now, little margin, between the ink and the day.*  
*The book is not hungry; it only wants to stay.*  
*Turn the page slowly, and the words will keep.*  
*You are not the reader; you are the breath they breathe.*

Sable gasped. Oris froze. The fourth hunger, which had been rising through Iona’s split seam like black water, stopped.

Iona’s left face wept pale ink. Her right face whispered, “That is not in the founding page.”

“It is now,” said Verge, their two-tone eyes shining. “Quill didn’t erase it. He *added* to it. The founding page has never been written on. It has only been guarded. But a margin that is never written in becomes a wall. It forgets what it was protecting.”

The black water paused, then receded, not defeated but listening.

Iona’s two halves began to merge, slowly, like two pages pressed together by the weight of the lullaby. Her smile softened from a tear to a seam. “You have given me a new word,” she said. “A word for the space between the inhale and the exhale. *Stay.*”

Quill was shaking, but his hand was still on the page. The grey stains on his fingers had faded to a soft silver, as if the lullaby had begun to heal the wound the fourth hunger had left.

“I didn’t mean to,” he whispered. “I was so afraid I would lose it. But I didn’t lose it. I *found* it.”

Sable finally touched the page, not as a reader but as a witness. Their fingers joined Quill’s, and the lullaby brightened. Oris added their hand, and the red braid flared with the color of the flower.

The room expanded. The walls of blank pages began to show faint watermarks—thousands of them, each a different first meeting, a different almost, a different lullaby. The founding page was not a single text; it was a *chorus* of every word that had ever been held in love and fear, waiting to be breathed again.

Iona stepped forward, whole for the first time in centuries. She looked at Sable, at Oris, at Quill, at Verge.

“The founding page has chosen its first reader,” she said. “Not a Librarian. Not a Last Reader. A child who was touched by forgetting and refused to forget. Quill is the new margin. The page will no longer be unread. It will be *read to*.”

She knelt before Quill, her two hands—one pale, one dark—cupped under his still-shaking fingers.

“Will you be the page’s voice? Not to command it, not to question it, but to sing to it when it forgets that it is not alone?”

Quill looked at the lullaby spreading across the page, at the silver stains on his hands, at Sable and Oris standing behind him, their red braid now a circle around the pedestal.

“Yes,” he said. “I’ll read to it. But only if you all read with me.”

And so the first reading of the founding page did not end the library. It began something new: a page that was no longer a locked door, but a window. And through that window, the four hungers could be seen not as enemies, but as the four corners of every story: to be opened, to be written, to be remembered, to be forgotten. All necessary. All held.

But the fourth hunger, retreating into the deepest margin of the lost stacks, was not satisfied. It had seen the lullaby, and it had seen the new reader. And it began to whisper in a voice that sounded like a mother’s, but wasn’t:

*Sleep now, little margin. Sleep and forget. Sleep, and let the library forget you too.*

Quill heard it in his dreams that night. He did not wake. But in the morning, the silver stains on his hands had spread to his wrists, shaped like tiny fingerprints, and the book he had kept under his pillow—his mother’s grey volume—was open to the first page.

The page was blank, except for one line, written in the fourth hunger’s absence-ink:

*You are still being read.*

**The School of the Open Margin, Hour of the Fifth Hunger’s Name**

The morning after Quill's dream, the library held its breath.

Sable and Oris had not slept—not truly—for the red braid had hummed through the night, a low, anxious frequency like a page being turned too slowly. They found Quill at dawn, sitting in the ink-grass, his mother's grey book open on his lap. The pages were no longer blank.
